Slide mechanic recap: 
- Normal move: tap adjacent square. Cat moves there. Then dog moves 2 steps.
- Slide: within 700ms of the normal move, tap the cat's CURRENT square (the one it just moved to). Then dCol=0, dRow=0 (tapping cat's own square). The `slides` condition: last move exists, dCol===0 && dRow===0, now-last.at < 700, slide target = catCol + last.dCol, catRow + last.dRow, must be on board, not crate, not dog. If valid, cat slides there, moves+1, dog does NOT move.

So the sequence for a "double move": 
1. Tap adjacent square B (cat moves A→B). Dog moves 2 steps (responding to cat at B).
2. Quickly tap B again (within 700ms). Cat slides B→C (same direction as A→B). Dog does NOT move.

Result: cat moved A→B→C (2 squares in a straight line), dog moved 2 squares (responding to cat at B, before the slide).

So per "double move", cat effectively moves 2, dog moves 2. But the timing: the dog moves after step 1 (cat at B), and the slide happens after. So the dog's 2 steps are computed based on cat at B, not C.

This is powerful but timing-sensitive (700ms). As an AI clicking, I need to click B then click B again within 700ms. That's doable if I batch them, but the second click must register as a separate click on the same cell. The distance from C to D': the cat moved 2 away, dog moved 2 toward B. If A→B→C is directly away from the dog, then B is 1 further from D than A, and C is 2 further. The dog moves 2 toward B, so D' is 2 closer to B than D.

Dog moves 2 toward B: dist(D', B) = dist(D,B) - 2 = d+1-2 = d-1. dist(D', C) = dist(D', B) + 1 (since C is 1 further from B than... wait, C is in the same direction as B from A, so C is 1 beyond B). dist(D', C) = dist(D', B) + dist(B, C) = (d-1) + 1 = d. So the distance stays the same!

But actually, wait. There's another concern. Let me re-read the slide condition:

```js
const last = JSON.parse(sessionStorage.getItem("lechonk_last_move") || "null");
const now = Date.now();
const slideCol = catCol + (last ? last.dCol : 0);
const slideRow = catRow + (last ? last.dRow : 0);
const slides = last && dCol === 0 && dRow === 0 && now - last.at < 700 &&
  slideCol >= 1 && slideCol <= 7 && slideRow >= 1 && slideRow <= 7 && !isCrate(slideCol, slideRow) &&
  !(slideCol === dogCol && slideRow === dogRow);
sessionStorage.removeItem("lechonk_last_move");
```

So `last` is the previous move's direction. After click 1 (normal move), `lechonk_last_move` = {dCol, dRow, at}. After click 2 (tap cat's square), dCol=0, dRow=0 (tapping cat's own square). `slides` = true if within 700ms and slide target valid. Then `sessionStorage.removeItem` clears it. So only ONE slide per normal move. Good.

But here's the thing: after click 1 (normal move), the code sets `sessionStorage.setItem("lechonk_last_move", ...)`. Then the dog moves. Then click 2 comes in. The code reads `last` = the direction from click 1. dCol=0, dRow=0 (click 2 is on cat's square). `slides` = true (if within 700ms). Cat slides. Good.

Wait, but there's a subtlety. After click 1, the cat is at B. The `lechonk_last_move` stores the direction A→B. When click 2 taps B (cat's square), the code computes dCol = tapCol - catCol = B.col - B.col = 0, dRow = 0. So dCol=0, dRow=0. And `last` = {dCol: direction, dRow: direction}. slideCol = catCol + last.dCol = B.col + direction.col = C.col. slideRow = B.row + direction.row = C.row. So the slide target is C = B + direction. ✓. Correct.

But between them, does the game process the first click fully (including the dog's move) before the second click? In a web app, the clicks are dispatched as separate events. The game processes them sequentially. The first click triggers the normal move (cat moves, dog moves, sessionStorage updated). The second click triggers the slide check (reads sessionStorage, finds the last move, checks timing). Since both happen in quick succession (within the same tool call), the timing should be fine.

If the two clicks are processed within, say, 50ms of each other, this is fine. But if there's any delay (e.g., the tool call takes time to dispatch the second click), it might exceed 700ms. To be safe, I'll batch them in one tool call.

The slide condition:
```
const last = JSON.parse(sessionStorage.getItem("lechonk_last_move") || "null");
const now = Date.now();
const slideCol = catCol + (last ? last.dCol : 0);
const slideRow = catRow + (last ? last.dRow : 0);
const slides = last && dCol === 0 && dRow === 0 && now - last.at < 700 &&
  slideCol >= 1 && slideCol <= 7 && slideRow >= 1 && slideRow <= 7 && !isCrate(slideCol, slideRow) &&
  !(slideCol === dogCol && slideRow === dogRow);
sessionStorage.removeItem("lechonk_last_move");
```

So: `last` is set when a normal move happens (stores dCol, dRow, at=now). On the NEXT tap, if that tap is the cat's own square (dCol=0,dRow=0) AND within 700ms of `last.at`, the cat slides by (last.dCol, last.dRow) from its current position.

Important: the slide direction is the direction of the LAST NORMAL MOVE. And the slide target = current cat position + last move direction. And it must not be a crate or the dog.

Also note: `sessionStorage.removeItem` happens regardless (after computing slides). So after any tap, last_move is cleared. So the slide can only happen on the tap IMMEDIATELY following a normal move.

So the pattern per turn:
1. Click adjacent square X (normal move). Cat → X. Dog moves 2. last_move = {dir, at}.
2. Within 700ms, click X (cat's current square). dCol=0,dRow=0, within 700ms → slide. Cat → X + dir. Dog doesn't move. last_move cleared.

Then next turn repeat.

The challenge: step 2 must happen within 700ms of step 1. Since I'm doing discrete clicks with screenshots in between, 700ms is very tight. The tool calls have latency. This might be hard to achieve reliably through this interface.

Actually, hold on. Let me reconsider the slide mechanic feasibility. The key question: can I reliably execute a slide? The slide requires tapping the cat's square within 700ms of the normal move. If I batch two clicks in one tool-call turn, they execute back-to-back rapidly. The question is whether the game processes them as two separate taps within 700ms.

Given the game is a web app, two rapid clicks should both register as separate click events, processed in quick succession (well within 700ms). So batching [click X, click X] should work: first click = normal move (sets last_move), second click = slide (within 700ms).

Actually, wait wait wait. Let me reconsider. I want to be careful about the slide execution. Let me reconsider whether batching two clicks reliably produces a slide. There's a risk that the two clicks are processed but the second one's `now - last.at < 700` fails if there's any delay, OR the second click is treated as a normal tap on an adjacent square (but it's the cat's own square, so dCol=0,dRow=0, which is the slide condition, not "move one square" — the "move one square" requires |dCol|+|dRow|===1). So tapping the cat's own square always goes to the slide check. If last_move is null or stale, then `slides` is false, and it falls to the `else` → message = "Le Chonk can only move one square". So a stale self-tap does nothing (just shows a message). Safe but no slide.

So the risk with batching: if the second click is too slow (>700ms), it just shows "can only move one square" and no slide. Then I've wasted the slide but the cat is still safe (at the normal-move destination). So it's not catastrophic — I just lose the slide for that turn. But then the dog has moved 2 and the cat only 1, which might be fatal.

Hmm. So the slide timing is critical and risky. Let me think about how to maximize the chance of the slide working.

When I batch [click X, click X], the two clicks are sent in one tool turn. The system executes them in order. The time between them should be small (just the time to dispatch two click events). In a web app, two click events dispatched back-to-back are processed within milliseconds. So `now - last.at` should be well under 700ms. So the slide should trigger reliably.

But there's another concern: after the first click, does the game state update (cat moves, dog moves, last_move set) BEFORE the second click is processed? In a web app, click events are processed sequentially by the event loop. The first click's handler runs (updating state, setting last_move), then the second click's handler runs (reading last_move). Since they're separate events processed in order, the state should be updated. So the slide should work.

But actually, there's a subtle issue: the game logic (gameStep) is a NodeGX JavaScriptFunction. When the first click triggers it, it runs and updates the variables (catCol, etc.) and sets sessionStorage. The second click triggers it again, reading the updated catCol and the sessionStorage. This should work if the events are processed sequentially. 

However, there's a risk that the two clicks are dispatched so fast that the second click's event is queued before the first click's handler completes... but JavaScript is single-threaded, so the first handler completes before the second starts. So it should be fine.

OK, I'm fairly confident batching [click X, click X] will produce a slide. But let me hedge: I'll do the normal move + slide as a batch, then observe the result. If the slide worked (cat moved 2), great. If not (cat moved 1, message shown), I'll adapt.

Wait, actually, I want to reconsider one thing: the slide mechanic and whether batching two identical clicks is the right way. Let me re-read the slide condition once more:

```
const last = JSON.parse(sessionStorage.getItem("lechonk_last_move") || "null");
const now = Date.now();
const slideCol = catCol + (last ? last.dCol : 0);
const slideRow = catRow + (last ? last.dRow : 0);
const slides = last && dCol === 0 && dRow === 0 && now - last.at < 700 &&
  slideCol >= 1 && slideCol <= 7 && slideRow >= 1 && slideRow <= 7 && !isCrate(slideCol, slideRow) &&
  !(slideCol === dogCol && slideRow === dogRow);
sessionStorage.removeItem("lechonk_last_move");
```

So `last` is read from sessionStorage. It's set by a normal move: `sessionStorage.setItem("lechonk_last_move", JSON.stringify({ dCol: dCol, dRow: dRow, at: now }))`.

Cat at row k, dog at row k+d (dog below, larger row number). Cat moves up (row decreases): cat → k-1. Dog moves up 2: dog → k+d-2. New gap = (k+d-2) - (k-1) = d-1. So the cat wins if k-1 < d, i.e., the cat reaches row 1 before the gap closes. 

So if the cat enters column 7 at (7,7) with the dog at distance d below, the cat needs 6 moves to reach (7,1) (from row 7 to row 1). The gap closes by 1 per turn, so the cat survives if d > 6, i.e., the dog is more than 6 rows below. But the dog can't be 6 rows below if it's chasing. So in a straight column-7 chase, the cat loses unless it has a big lead or slides.

With slides: each round the cat moves 2 up (normal + slide), dog moves 2 up (after normal). Net gap change: cat +2 up, dog +2 up → gap unchanged! So with slides, the cat maintains the gap while moving 2/round. So if the cat enters column 7 with any positive gap, slides let it maintain the gap and reach the fish. That's why the slide strategy works.

Let me think about which is more likely to work. In these web-game agent setups, a "batch" of actions in one function_calls block are executed sequentially by the browser automation, typically with small delays (maybe 50-200ms between actions). The 700ms window should be satisfiable. And state propagation: each click is a separate DOM event; Noodl processes the first click's signal chain (gameStep → variables) before or during the second click's event. Since they're separate events, the first should complete its synchronous processing before the second fires. So Option A (batched) is more likely to work.

Let me reconsider: the slide is triggered by clicking the cat's own square. But what if, instead of relying on the 700ms window across two tool calls, I batch the two clicks in ONE tool call? The tool executes click 1 then click 2 in quick succession. The 700ms window is from click 1's gameStep (`at: now`) to click 2's gameStep (`now - last.at < 700`). If both clicks are in the same batch, the time between them is small (the automation dispatches them back-to-back, maybe 10-100ms apart). So <700ms. Good.

State propagation: click 1's gameStep runs, outputs catCol/catRow, which update the variables. Then click 2's gameStep runs, reads the updated variables. In Noodl, the function runs synchronously and its outputs are written to variables. The next click event (click 2) is a separate event; by the time it fires, the variables should be updated (Noodl processes signals synchronously within an event, and the next event sees the updated state). So click 2 should see cat at (2,7).

But wait — there's a subtle issue with batching two clicks on the SAME cell. The first click moves the cat to (2,7) and the dog to (3,5). The second click is on (2,7). For the slide to trigger, the second click's gameStep must see catCol=2, catRow=7 (updated). If the batch processes both clicks before state updates, the second click sees catCol=1, catRow=7 (old), dCol=2-1=1, dRow=0 → nextToCat → normal move again (cat (1,7)→(2,7), already there, dog moves again). That would be bad.

So the batch MUST have state propagation between the two clicks. This is the risk. 

To mitigate: maybe I should add a tiny delay between the two clicks? But I can't add delays within a batch easily. The `wait` action could add a delay, but that would exceed 700ms. Hmm.

Alternatively, maybe the two clicks in a batch ARE processed as separate events with state updates in between (since each click is a separate DOM event dispatched by the automation). In that case, state propagates. I think this is likely — browser automation dispatches each click as a separate event, and Noodl processes each event's signal chain synchronously before the next event. So state should propagate.
